ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U1	We have come to agenda item 13. The second reading of the draft law amending the Law on study grants and student loans initiated by the Government of the Republic and the Tax Administration Act begins. Priit Sibula, Member of the Cultural Commission of the Speaking Chair of the Riigikogu, for presentation!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U2	Honourable Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu! Ladies and gentlemen and guests! First of all, I still consider myself green, both as a politician and as onion. What do I mean by that? I hope that if I pass on the basics of the debate in the Commission, our debate here will go constructively and quickly. I will start by saying that I think it would be appropriate to thank the chairman of the committee and all the members of the committee, because together we have made this good bill even better in my opinion. I recall that the first reading took place on 26 September and that the deadline for submitting amendments was 12 October. The changes were presented by the Social Democrats, the Centre Party, the Association of Estonian Student Unions, Jaak Allik, member of the Riigikogu, and jointly by the student communities of the University of Tartu, Tallinn University of Technology and Estonian University of Life Sciences. Before and after the first reading, the Committee on Culture asked the opinion of the Association of Estonian Students Unions, the Association of Estonian Parents, the Council of Rectors, the Constitutional, Social and Legal Affairs Committee of the Riigikogu and the Legal and Analytical Department of the Chancellery of the Riigikogu. We discussed this draft seven times in committee. In addition, both I and the chairman of the committee met several interested parties, including students. Of course, a representative of the initiator of the draft took part in the meetings of the Commission. Several amendments were proposed to the draft. Some of the most important things I'm talking about now. One of them intended to lower the requirement for 100% completion of the curriculum to 85% under the conditions of need-based learning support. The initiator and the majority of the Commission did not support this proposal. According to the founder of the bill, learning without reimbursement of study costs and need-based learning support are a prerequisite for a higher education institution to create all the opportunities for the student to study. The study volume is not automatically 30 credits, but the university provides a volume and allows it to be completed. Similarly, it is not possible to predict the effects of completing a 85% curriculum. This may become an informed choice for the student, and therefore the concept of free higher education becomes inconsistent. Several proposals were taken into account in the draft and several amendments were made. First of all, I am talking about differentiation of subsidies. There was still one step in the draft, with a grant of EUR 135 per student. The Commission has now proposed that if the income per family member is up to EUR 70, the student allowance will be EUR 220, if the income is between EUR 70 and EUR 140, the allowance will be EUR 135 and if the income is between EUR 140,01 and EUR 280, the student allowance will be EUR 75. If the income per family member is more than 280 euros, it is not possible to apply for the allowance. Secondly, the need-based education allowance does not count as income when calculating income support. The third important decision is that a student of up to 24 years of age is considered to be related to his or her parents when calculating the need-based education allowance. So far, the bill had this age limit of 26 years. I would remind you that the deadline for submitting proposals was 12 October. However, on Friday, 30 November, the Student Association had a proposal asking for grants to be differentiated by 10 euros. However, as I said, neither the initiator of the draft nor the majority of the Commission could discuss this proposal because it was submitted too late. I spoke to the head of the Student Association. He said that they simply wanted to pay further attention to this and that they knew that they had submitted this proposal later than the deadline. The Commission also took procedural decisions. Firstly, it was decided to send the draft to second reading on 5 December. This decision was consensual, but it is now December 6th, December 5th. Secondly, it was proposed to close the second reading. There were 6 members of the Commission in favour, no one opposed, with 2 abstentions. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U3	Thank you, good waitress. You have questions. Priit Toobal, please!

At that time, the Minister of Education and Research commented on this amendment and said that he did not know what the arguments of the banks were, why a 66-year-old could not be, but a very timid lady would no longer be a guarantor of student loans. Has the Commission discussed this issue in greater depth and discussed it again between two readings? What are the results of the discussion, why can Grandma no longer be the guarantor of her grandson's student loan?
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U5	Thank you. We met between two readings with representatives of both the Ministry of Finance and the Ministry of Education and Research. We discussed the subject of student loans in depth and we will definitely come back to this issue.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U25	Thank you, Mr President-in-Office. Dear waitress, It is undoubtedly gratifying that this draft has taken into account a number of proposals that have also been put forward by the Social Democrats. For example, the fact that this allowance is not included in income when calculating income support is also below the age limit. But here's the question. In Estonia, different laws are based on different incomes: in the case of child benefit based on needs, the income of previous months is taken as the basis, income for the previous month in the case of income support, and data for the income tax return for the previous year in the case of income support based on needs. However, it may happen that the student's parents or one parent, for example, have lost their job six months ago when the student went to school. Doesn't it seem to you that this foundation is not very well founded?
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U26	Jaak Allik, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U27	Thank you. The problem you referred to may be one of the most serious of this bill. The Commission has dealt with this quite thoroughly. The universities were given an additional opportunity, which is also included in the explanatory memorandum. Universities can deal with such critical exceptional cases and it is reasonable that it takes place as close to the students as possible, i.e. it is handled by the university. Whether universities delegate this task to agencies or do so by rectors, it remains up to the university itself.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U94	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, Honourable guests! Estonia exists because of the education of its people. Statistically, the emergence of such a small country was considered unlikely. Repeated discussions have been held on how he developed, looking for reasons and reasons. Those who long doubted that such a country could even arise, although it existed, eventually realised that there was one argument that was not taken into account. This was the 'abnormal' effort of Estonians towards education and knowledge. It seems to me that by now the vast majority of this belief in education has simply been eradicated. I dare say that, in a relative sense, universities and higher education have never been regarded as less important in Estonia than in other respects. Indeed, there were major disputes during the discussion of a specific draft law. It has to be acknowledged that the Commission has worked hard. For example, the same subject that was already discussed here by my colleague on Onion, the age to which a student could be associated with his parents, being a member of this family, to the age of 21, 24 or 26. I think that the Commission has reached some compromise by finding that this age is not 26 years old, as the Ministry had proposed. There was a long discussion about how the study grants still relate to 100% educational success. If someone is missing a point, will they lose all possible support? The question of whether the student's residence should be taken into account was discussed in depth. There were very constructive discussions in the Commission. The experience of universities is that if, for example, travel grants or something else are determined by their place of residence, everyone will sign themselves out of Tallinn or Tartu and sign themselves in somewhere very far away. So there were a number of problems. There was also a discussion about whether students themselves could start solving the issue of student support and whether student associations should not share money. In the end, the students also found that it may be dangerous to take on the task of allocating insufficient funding, because it is the people who share the money that are responsible for the lack of money. In fact, however, the main problem related to this law is as follows. This is a general problem for our Parliament and our country. There's a difference between making laws or making laws. This law is linked to several other laws on university reform. This relates to the work of Parliament as a whole. Have you noticed that every time education or research policy is discussed, everyone is in favour of talking about the importance of universities, research-intensive production and other great things? But when the budget comes to the end, they say, "Where do you take the money? There's just no money! " This is the tree of the knowledge of good and bad. We are discussing this box that has been given, and we are not really going to get to politics. To me, the Estonian Parliament is increasingly reminding of the branch office of a large global company or, rather, its accounting, where it is calculated well, the work is done well, the tables are done well, only that everything basic has been decided somewhere else. So is our education policy. It was said that the total amount of study grants would double. Awesome! At the same time, it is the effect of a beginner's stamp collector. If a beginner collects four brands and he gets four more, he doubles the number of brands. If someone already has enough stamps to be considered as a stamp collection, 10000 brands, for example, and receives 100 brands, then the percentage increase is very low. This is also the case with student benefits. How good it is to raise it when there is almost no such support right now! The fact that money is insufficient and there's nowhere to take it is also a pretty bad thing. You know, the principles of budgeting that we're talking about here were last talked about in the 19th century. Then it was discussed that four manors were given to the University of Tartu, he could get them for himself and see how he could handle them. It's not like that these days. It is not credible for people in the coalition to say, 'Do you want to take money from pensioners or? Do you want to take money from the saviors? " In today's world, those who make practical decisions do not simply take from one box to another, but the budget is shaped through priorities. At times, very small percentages are taken from here and there, but these are large sums of money. This is the story of today's budget. Human capacity is the greatest national wealth. Of course, we have had positive efforts here. I admire our Minister for Education and Science, even in the sense that, given the current government, it is a big deal to double. But we need to look to the future and think about what happens next. There are major problems with funding universities. As a university professor, I dare say that we rely very much on euro money at all times. As far as scientific money is concerned, we have already decided something here, but the bulk of the money comes from somewhere else. If you think it doesn't involve any suggestions for using it, you're wrong. The risk of provincialisation is increasing. Another thing that relates to universities and education is what Priit Sibula and I had to talk about. I have to answer that question myself now. Compared to other developed European countries, the economic situation of our students after the adoption of this law is still weak. The run-off of young people is growing, and it already has a threatening scale. Not only do you go to school, you go to work. The worst-case scenario predicts that perhaps there will be up to 850000 people in Estonia in 2020. It is no longer a country-specific population; instead of a country, it is a province. Education also needs to remain at the same level as others. In education, if you stand still or almost still, the earth will rotate so fast that you will become more and more behind others. We have this threat. This means that it is wrong to simply value education and higher education in general; it simply requires more money. If we lose our belief in education, which is disproportionate to other countries, then we, as a small country, will simply not survive. We're in the province. It's nice to have foreign students. But do we really want what a Swedish journalist or political scientist predicted three weeks ago? That opinion went through our newspapers, too. He thought that if this were to happen, in 50 years' time, Estonia would be an average wealthy English province in Europe.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U95	Riigikogu speech chair Jaak Alliku, please! Eight minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U96	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, Honourable students! The views of the Social Democratic Party on need-based education support remain the same. We presented them in the spring, introducing our draft to the Riigikogu, which was essentially rejected by the government coalition without debate. We believe that the draft proposed by the government, which provides for a EUR 135 grant for students who should be dependent on their parents until the age of 26, with a household income not exceeding EUR 280 per person and requiring full completion of the curriculum, does not guarantee free higher education but, on the contrary, makes higher education very difficult for children of less-insured families. We are confident that in a few years' time, we will see that the opinion of Minister Aaviksoo, published in Sirp's newspaper, according to which the draft law on study grants and student loans transferred to the Riigikogu allows all students to devote themselves to full-time studies, regardless of the family's income, is inadequate to say the least. Once again, we stress that in Scandinavian countries, where there is really free higher education, the state's funding for higher education is worth 17 to 35% of the funding for education grants. We have a request from Minister Aaviksoo to increase this share from the current 5% to 9% by 2015, which alone shows that free higher education was only and will remain an IRL electoral lecture. The Social Democratic Party has proposed, in the course of the 2013 state budget procedure, to double the funding allocated to needs-based learning support. This proposal was rejected by the government coalition, which clearly shows that higher education is not a government priority. With regard specifically to draft 236, I am pleased to note that the Committee on Culture did indeed have a substantive discussion of the draft and a debate based on the desire to improve it. For this, I sincerely thank the chairman of the committee, Urmas Klaas. On several points, the bill has improved a lot. In particular, this decision shows that the education allowance is not included in the family income for the purposes of calculating income support. In this sense, the draft initially submitted was not to support students in need of student support, but rather to taunt them. Another important point is the reduction of the age of the so-called family relationship from 26 to 24 years of age, as envisaged by the proposal of the Association of Estonian Student Unions, which would actually link the age taken from the ceiling to the age prescribed by law in the case of a survivor's pension. The third change under the consensus was the offer, rather than a single €135 grant, of a 'grade', which would amount to €75, €135 and €220, depending on the student's family's "average income'. However, it seems to me that we rushed somewhat in making this decision, because the result is that more than half of those who are supposed to be entitled to support will receive EUR 75 instead of EUR 135. As a result, there will also be inequality, as for example, a student with EUR 270 'family allowance' could count on EUR 345 together with the student allowance, but only EUR 220 without any income. The Student Association proposed a differentiation of allowances to ensure that each person in need, together with the family allowance, would still receive EUR 280. To prove this, I would like to read to you in advance about the appeal of the Student Association to Parliament after the debate at second reading of the draft in the Committee on Culture had ended: 'Firstly, the application of the three rates of support as a step may not change the inequality in the financial situation of students enough to enable them to actually devote themselves to studies. We would like the Riigikogu to be proposed on the basis of steps of ten euros and not just three steps. This would ensure fair and needs-based support for those who need it. The offer currently on the table, in which less support can be obtained with the difference of one cent, is not a sufficient guarantee for students in need of support. The differences between levels of support are too large and the transition from one level of income to another is reflected in the drastic decline in support, which is not in line with the legal objective of ensuring students have equal opportunities to devote themselves to their studies. In our opinion, setting and paying a grant of ten euros with precision does not make the system much more bureaucratic and costly. From the perspective of the student, this type of support, as precise as possible, is once again more clearly linked to its economic situation and the need for support. ' The Social Democratic Party Group supports the proposal of the Estonian Student Association and, in this context, proposes to interrupt the second reading of Bill 236 so that we can introduce a larger and more needs-based differentiation of grants into the draft, which would not change the overall budget amount. Indeed, there was no mention in the Commission of the problem that Kadri Simson raised that if a student receives a scholarship, where it is supposed to belong. It gets pretty weird when a student gets a scholarship for particularly good learning outcomes, but is punished by withdrawing support for it. The law should also state that this will not happen. Finally, I will specify the number that was mentioned here, how many students would receive support. There is no need to be a witch, Nastya, just EUR 3.6 million, planned for next year, divided by 540. We are talking about four months and, as we know, we are only talking about the first semester and only those students who go to university. For them, success in learning is not important either, as those who meet the requirements will receive support. For some reason, the Ministry of Education is consistently of the opinion that 37% of students meet the requirements. So this number should be 6290 if you divide it exactly. Once again, when the press began to say that 16,000 students were going to receive support, the explanatory statement had been misunderstood. However, an exact number could go into the press. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U106	Dear Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu, Ladies and gentlemen, Members of the Riigikogu! Good students who listen so thoroughly to the bill that concerns you! First of all, let us look back at how we have had our student benefits in recent years. There have been several drafts on this subject. In the last ten years, there have been so many in the hall that it is not possible to count them on the fingers of two hands. The aim of all of these has been quite similar to what we are discussing today: how to give every young person who is capable enough the opportunity to study, receive higher education and this in the Republic of Estonia. When we look at the fate of the drafts on education grants, we see that almost everyone has ended quite sadly. They have either been reworded, modified, repeatedly withdrawn at source, or have at one point been reworded in such a way that their original point has been lost. This time we have come to a second reading. The Bureau's proposal is to reach a third reading on an accelerated basis. So maybe this time we'll adopt this bill as a law. However, the answer to a few questions from the Committee on Culture is that don't worry, we come back to it, we can change it, we can complement it, gives us a clear message that if, after four months of implementation, it comes out (after all, in 2013 it will only be applied for a short period of time and even for first-year students), that it is too burdensome for the state budget or that there is not so much resources, then it can be adjusted somewhere, removed from some of the three stages, or reworded to someone who is still receiving this support. It should be mentioned that this amendment, which was agreed in the Committee on Culture and which was supported by the government after it was astonished, is that student needs-based learning support is not included in the income support calculation. Otherwise, the situation would be that a young person who is so good that, despite the difficult conditions of his family, he or she can reach higher education must choose whether his or her family will continue to receive income support and whether his or her brothers and sisters will survive if he or she is studying in Tartu or Tallinn, for example. That would certainly not be fair. It is not certain how this went through in the Government of the Republic, whether Jürgen Ligi was out of the room at that time or whether the vote was otherwise held, but in any case, it is a pleasure to note that at least the grants for studies were not cancelled. I'm sorry about my skepticism, but I've been exposed to student grants many times before. I have dealt with quite a number of drafts on this subject in this Chamber, and I have also launched several drafts. Often, in the end, the excuse has been that there is no money, and that is why such an ambitious draft is not appropriate. Since the necessity-based teaching allowance logic is that all young people who comply with a certain framework will also receive this support, i.e. if these young people have a high unemployment rate for one or another sad reason, ongoing crises, redundancies or regional mismatches more than expected, these allowances should be provided with additional resources, as the rapporteur said, from reserves or elsewhere. However, life has often shown that it is easier to come to the Riigikogu, make minor changes and actually pull together among the beneficiaries. When looking at the writing of Urmas Klaas in Postimees yesterday, he stressed that it is estimated that there is currently a need-based system of study grants for 16540 people. The very first important proposal, which is actually the basis of all the necessity-based learning support logic, and which is certainly being voted on here today, is that student grants should apply not only to students who are just entering higher education but also to students who are already studying. See all analyses carried out by the Student Association and Research Institutions, including the Ministry of Education with the Student Association. They show that in a situation where unemployment is so high and so many social problems, only a very small percentage of students can fully commit to their studies. Over 80% of students are forced to work full-time and, unfortunately, not in professional employment. The goal is only to cover life's costs, and studies have often remained in second place. I myself have given lectures for quite many years, and I must say that it is very common for a student to apologise for having to postpone the exam because it is either the deadline for the accounts or the time for the annual reports. I believe that almost all the lecturers in this Chamber who have encountered it know that these students will be met. In Estonian society it is common for young people to work. Does the Need-Based Study Allowance Act bring us closer to the goal that students can actually devote themselves to studies? Given the amounts foreseen, I'm afraid I won't. Thank you.

Such distributions of money are always accompanied by a great deal of trouble and confusion. For example, there is a project such as 'Tonight live on the air', which is similarly financed from MISA resources and where you have the honour to participate, right, as well as interviewed. All these things have to do with creating tensions and, in my opinion, not very well with freedom of the media. However, as the European Union has taken such a position, we regretably have to live with it.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U251	We do not have separate funds in cooperation with any country within which to operate. It all goes according to the possibilities available. As we know, there are several sources in Estonia. In addition to the "Estonian Culture in the World" programme of the Ministry of Culture, we also have the Cultural Endowment of Estonia, through which such trips are financed. The text of this programme sets out the general principles of how funding is carried out. The principle is that the sending party pays travel expenses, such as travel expenses to and from Estonia to Ukraine, as well as the salaries of cultural figures. The receiving party shall pay for accommodation, domestic transport and translation services, if necessary. This is a general principle, which does not mean that different agreements are not possible for a given project. This general cooperation has now been agreed with the Ukrainians. In our relations with other countries, too, we do not consider it necessary to make separate funds, i.e. to set specific amounts of money, which could be counterproductive in that sense, that if more can be done, the question arises whether there is no possibility of more funding. Actually, it is.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U326	Good colleague and waitress! The draft refers to the European Convention for the Protection of Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms. ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U327	Thanks for the question, but, good Marika, spare me some patience, I have a whole bunch of paperwork here. I found it! How do I comment on that? It is a European Convention for the Protection of Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms, namely Article 6 thereof. This article is entitled 'Right to a fair trial'. I just read it out, it's short: 'Everyone has the right to a fair and public trial, within a reasonable period of time, of independence and impartiality in deciding on his or her civil rights and obligations or the criminal charges brought before him, in a court set up by law. The judgment shall be delivered in public. The intermediate paragraph is not very important in the current context. Next, 'Everyone accused of a crime is considered innocent until proven guilty in accordance with the law. 'The third paragraph is the most important point in Article 6: 'Every accused person shall have at least the following rights: (a) obtain in an immediately understandable language detailed information on the nature and grounds of the accusation against him; (b) have sufficient time and opportunity to prepare for his defence; (c) defend himself or through a lawyer of his choice, or obtain free legal assistance if this is necessary for the purposes of justice and the accused does not have sufficient means to pay for legal aid; (d) question himself or have witnesses called to testify against him be questioned, have witnesses summoned from his side and be questioned on the same terms as witnesses who testify against him; (e) use of free interpreter's assistance if he does not understand or speak the language used in court. ' If we look at this small list, this point (e) which guarantees the assistance of an interpreter when a person does not speak the language used in court, and the right to state legal aid should be extended to all, not only to those referred to in his reply by the honourable Minister Kristen Michal, provided that everyone has learned the Estonian language. Not all of us have learned Estonian! We know this very well, we are constantly measuring our degree of integration and preparing integration plans. We know what the situation really is. I think this convention has a direct link to the draft we are debating today.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U346	Please, colleague Ester Tuiksoo, the second question!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U347	Dear waitress, good colleague. You have really dealt with the very essence of the matter, and you certainly have information on the extent to which other countries of the European Union, especially those who joined us at the same time, have provided foreign-language legal assistance to their citizens.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U348	I am now very tempted to read the long answer that Lauri Laas received yesterday. But I believe it's in the verbatim if anyone's interested. In your question, you are referring specifically to state legal aid, i.e. legal aid, the cost of which is paid by the state. In the countries of the European Union, the criterion for providing this is that the person's economic situation does not allow him or her to pay the costs of legal aid himself. If the recipient of the legal aid does not speak the official language, the necessary translation costs shall also be payable in the context of the legal aid. It is an iron rule in every country of the European Union. In the event of a cross-border dispute concerning the provision of national legal aid, it follows from Article 7 of Council Directive 2003/8/EC that legal aid granted in the Member State in which the court is situated covers the costs of interpretation and also the costs of translating the documents required by the court or other competent authority and to be provided by the recipient of the legal aid which are necessary for the settlement of the case. There is another factor at stake here, which is also discussed in the Ministry's opinion. They claim that this is not a problem at all, because everywhere in Estonia there are so-called legal pharmacies, where any Russian-speaking person can go and have this application completed. This is not quite true for the simple reason that legal pharmacies are not everywhere in Estonia. For example, there is a courthouse in Pärnu, but there is and will not be a legal pharmacy. And even if he does, there will most likely be a single lawyer who doesn't speak Russian. In this sense, it is evil to delegate this problem to a third sector.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U349	Please, Mr Tarmo Tamm, the second question!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U350	Good waitress! In order for you to be able to fully convince me, I'll ask you if you've turned to the Chancellor of Justice's office. ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U351	I haven't done it yet. To be honest, I haven't considered it at first either. ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U371	Good President! Ladies and gentlemen, The draft law amending the State Legal Aid Act 271 was discussed in the Legal Affairs Committee on 13 November. a. The meeting was chaired by Marko Pomerants and attended by Andres Anvelt, Jaanus Tamkivi as an alternate member of Igor Gräzin, Kalle Jents, Marika Tuus-Laul, Neeme Suur, Peeter Võsa, Siim Kabrits, Valdo Randpere, as well as advisor Linnar Liivamägi and adviser-secretary director Raini Laide. There was no Kalle Laane, protocol Tiina Kärsna. The representative of the Estonian Central Party Group, Yana Toom, was also invited and Gunnar Vaikmaa, adviser to the Ministry of Justice's supervisory and legal services service, was invited. The Riigikogu Member of the Estonian Centre Party Group and the preparer of the draft Yana Toom gave the committee a comprehensive overview of the need to amend the State Legal Aid Act. He described the content and objectives of the draft and, of course, answered the questions. Yana Toom explained that the draft is needed to bring the form of the application for state legal aid into line with the European Convention for the Protection of Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms and the Constitution of the Republic of Estonia. He explained that since, on average, one in three people in Estonia has Russian as their native language, it would be necessary for the initial legal aid to be available to these people in that language as well. The aim is to ensure equal treatment for all people living in Estonia and to provide Russian as the language of the application for legal aid in the State Legal Aid Act, in addition to Estonian and English. He explained that it was older people who were affected by the problem. Acquiring the Estonian language is no longer feasible for many of them. Yana Toom stressed that the state should treat all residents equally and referred to both the Constitution and the European Convention for the Protection of Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms. The main changes to the draft are as follows: Firstly, all residents of Estonia will be given the opportunity to ask for legal aid from the state, and secondly, conditions will be created to help those who need the state's legal aid in real terms. There was also a historical background. According to Statistics Estonia, 109 languages are spoken as a native language in Estonia, but 97% of the residents consider Estonian or Russian as their native language. The majority of representatives of other smaller ethnic groups also speak Russian as their native language, with the exception of Swedes, Finns, Gypsies and people of some other nationalities who consider Estonian as their native language. In addition, it is known from the recent census and also from sociological studies that a significant part of the local population does not speak the Estonian language well or knows it at all. This is confirmed by the results of the 2008 monitoring of the integration of Estonian society. Particularly serious problems, as has been said, relate to pensioners and other older people. Article 6, point 3, of the ECHR provides that any accused person must have the right to defend himself or to obtain legal aid through a lawyer of his choice, unless the interests of justice so require and the accused does not have enough money to pay for this legal aid. In addition, the obligation of the Republic of Estonia to ensure the availability of state legal aid to all residents is also due to the combined effects of §§ 12, 13, 15 and 28 of the Constitution. Under the Constitution, the state must treat everyone before the law as equal and guarantee everyone equal opportunities for the protection of the law and the state, including the possibility of recourse to the courts to defend their rights. The Riigikogu discussed for the first time, as has already been said here, the draft law on state legal aid in 2004. Paragraph 12 (5) of that Act, which deals with the form of the application and the content of the application, was worded at the time in such a way that if the application or the documents attached to it were not submitted in Estonian, the person processing the application would arrange for the translation of those documents. If the application for legal aid is rejected, the costs of translating the documents shall be recognised by the applicant, while the costs of translating the documents shall be taken into account in determining the obligations of the recipient of the legal aid. According to the explanatory memorandum to the draft act, the aim was to ensure that all persons had a professional and reliable legal service in a timely, adequate and accessible manner. On 15 June 2005, the Act implementing the Code of Civil Procedure and the Code of Enforcement Procedure was adopted and entered into force on 1 January 2006, which laid down § 12 (5) of the Law on Legal Aid in the new version. It is worded as follows: 'The application for state legal aid is submitted in Estonian. The application may also be made in English if the legal aid is requested by a natural person residing in another Member State of the European Union or who is a national of another Member State of the European Union, or by a legal person established in another Member State of the European Union. The application forwarded to the court in another language shall be returned. ' The representative of the Ministry of Justice then presented the position of the Government of Vaikmaa and stated that the Government did not support the draft. He also answered questions from the members of the Commission and from the initiator's representative. Why does the Government of the Republic not support this bill, and what is the government's opinion? On 11 October this year, the government decided unanimously not to support the draft 271 initiated by the Group of the Estonian Centre Party. That's for the next reason. This draft law justifies the need to amend Paragraph 12 (5) of the Law on Legal Aid with reference to Article 6 (3) of the ECHR. In the view of the Government, the draft does not contain any examples of how Article 12 (5) of the State Legal Aid Act in force contradicts this document, either on the basis of that convention or on the basis of the case-law of the European Court of Human Rights. The initiator of the draft law claims that a situation in which an application for legal aid can also be filed in English in the Estonian language and in the cases provided for by the law may result in a breach of the fundamental right to universal equality laid down in § 12 of the Constitution. At the same time, the Supreme Court has stated that restricting the principle of equality is not in itself a violation of this principle and that the principle of equality in lawmaking has been violated when unequal treatment cannot be justified in constitutional terms. According to the practice of the Supreme Court, it is sufficient that there is a reasonable, relevant reason to justify unequal treatment, which is not contrary to the Constitution. The government also points out that § 6 of the Constitution states that Estonian is the official language. The use of Estonian as an official language is further regulated by § 51 and 52 of the Constitution. According to § 52 (1) of the Constitution, the working language of state institutions and local governments is Estonian. Administration is all official communication that must then be used in Estonian. The Government of the Republic considers that if the law on state legal aid is amended in such a way as to allow the application for legal aid to be made in Russian as well, the translation of applications will place too great a burden on the state, which is not feasible. It is recommended to seek legal aid from non-profit organisations or legal advice agencies free of charge. It is noted that the Supreme Court has stated that according to § 12 (5) of the Legal Aid Act, the application for legal aid must be submitted in Estonian or English; the application submitted in another language will be returned because there should be a uniform system of signs. Pursuant to § 12 (5) of the Law on Legal Aid, an application for legal aid may also, in certain cases, be submitted in English. This is when a person stays abroad and needs such legal assistance. The government has also noted that English is the language most spoken in the European Union, which is also known by the majority of Estonian officials. Coalition politicians and government representatives generally pointed out that the courts already have a lot of work and this legislative amendment would bring more work. It was also claimed that there are no Russian speakers in the courts, in my view this is not the case, and that, since the application is multi-page, long and voluminous, translating them would be very laborious. In fact, Yana Toom here showed that the initial application for legal aid is only a few pages long. It was also noted that the majority of the courts speak English in addition to Estonian. The main counter argument, however, was the need for additional work and additional expenditure. A number of questions were asked in the Commission. If the rejection of the draft was largely justified by the fact that Estonian is the official language of Estonia, why does the law also allow the application to be completed in English? Whether it's a matter of principle was asked. In reply, according to Article 12 (5) of the Law on Legal Aid, the application for legal aid may also be submitted in English in certain cases on the grounds that the possibilities for translation in Estonian by citizens of the European Union living abroad are much more limited than those who speak Russian in Estonia. There was an argument that was already mentioned here, but I'm still talking about it. In the Commission, it was argued that it was like a political decision, there was also talk of a sneaky russification. They asked how many Russian-speaking people were left without help. There aren't any statistics on that, but I realized a couple of dozen people. It's just that the state has no interest in it and hasn't kept any statistics. There was also a discussion of how well an Estonian would be able to fill out these important and complex legal documents to help the Russian people. It would still be necessary for the content of the application to be further understood, understood, what is the problem at all. If the form is not understood and the person in need does not know how to write his story thoroughly, then the whole process may depend on it. In the Commission, an example was given by Minister Aaviksoo, who passed the Estonian language exam, as we remember, and who could not say whether he made a few minor mistakes there or not. It was stressed that such language support would be essential. A good colleague, Valdo Randpere, pointed out in the example of Sweden that there is no foreign language assistance in Sweden, for example, when exchanging passports. He suggested that you learn Estonian so that we do not have to bully and fight. There was also talk about the survival of Estonian language and culture, a little like in the context of the political lecture. Yana Toom replied that the Estonian language should be the official language, and that is what you should speak very well. But he represents his constituents and, unfortunately, they do not all speak Estonian and so they have problems. He added that the Finnish language can also be handled very well at the Swedish courthouse and can submit documents in Finnish. It was further argued that if the applicant had a lawyer, there would be no language problem. However, a person should be able to apply for primary state aid in his or her native language. We shouldn't consciously screw up the tension, and we could still meet people. The need for help is often elderly people who are unable to learn the Estonian language. That's a pretty detailed overview, then. He was told exactly what happened in the committee, almost like a verbatim. The draft would enter into force as a law in January 2013. The Commission decided to put this draft on the agenda for the plenary session by 4 December 2012, i.e. today, the decision was consensual. The Commission proposes to reject the draft, with 5 Commission members in favour, 4 against and no abstentions. So it's pretty close. The rapporteur in the Chamber was appointed by consensus to speak here. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U376	Madam President, I would like to thank you very much. Colleagues! We are well acquainted with socialism in Estonia more than 20 years ago. But what was wasn't complete socialism. Communism would never have been achieved, for what theorists had invented was the perfect model. Communism was a characteristic of striving for equality in the development of society, for neither the poor nor the rich. It was the perfect model. But life brings in corrections. When theory reaches practice, when trying to create this ideal model somewhere in the country creates collective farms where, of course, there is no equality, there is a clear understanding that it should be done differently. What, then, is the ideal model of the national language? I don't think you're gonna take this as a joke, but if you think about it, the perfect model would be a model where everyone living in Estonia would speak Estonian. In fact, if we were talking about the ideal model, they would only speak Estonian for what would more languages be needed? For the ideal model, the entire written word from books to the Internet would be in Estonian only, and all the information that spreads in the country would be in Estonian. Information coming into the country would only be in Estonian and out of the country as well. That would be the perfect model. We understand that such a thing cannot be achieved, even if there were 10 million Estonians, as the President of the Riigikogu offered in one of his interviews, answering the question of how many of us should be. But even then, that would be unlikely. I discussed this with a colleague who left us a long time and a half ago before he realized that this was a purely theoretical approach. But it took time! And judging by the serious faces in the hall, I assume the idiocy of the perfect model has never been told to you before. I hope you understand that there is something in this model that does not allow it to be carried out. In short, ideal models exist, but it is impossible to realise the ideal model in practice. This knowledge gives us the opportunity to approach real life and look at the problem addressed in bill 271 from both a pragmatic and a human perspective. A specific person turns to the state for legal aid. Is he doing this for entertainment? No, she does it for him in a difficult situation when she needs a quick response. As a rule, this person is in a poor financial situation. Given that we are talking about the Russian-speaking population, it can really be a rule. The choice of the country is either to insist on reaching out to the ideal model or to base itself on humanistic principles, humanity that everyone understands. That means that today, if we're realists, if we're mostly human pragmatists here, we should support this bill. If not, it is likely to be voted against. But don't do that! Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U389	The next item is the third reading of the draft law amending the Law on the Bar Association and other Acts initiated by the Government of the Republic. Do the representatives of the political groups wish to negotiate? Please, Marika Tuus-Laul as representative of the Centre Party Group.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U390	President-in-Office of the Council. Ladies and gentlemen, This draft law aims to amend the organisational arrangements of the Bar Association, which abolish the status of parental assistant of the lawyer, amend the form of the barrister's examination and align the existing law with the Directive of the European Parliament and of the Council on more effective guarantee of the freedom to provide legal services. In the Commission, the debate on the draft law has been quite meaningful. The author has repeatedly emphasised that the draft law has also been discussed at the General Assembly of the Estonian Bar Association. Lawyers have agreed and agreed to the draft. I would therefore rather focus on a directive aimed at amending the regulation on advertising bans. Under the current law, lawyers are not allowed to advertise their activities in any way. So far, one of the main arguments of the ban has been that public advertising can lead to a situation where lawyers are to be chosen purely according to advertising banners, which do not coincide with more conservative views, for example, on the specific nature of the profession. At the same time, advocacy is a good way to inform a large number of potential clients about the services offered by the law firm. Thus, people can learn more about which law offices operate in different fields. This amendment is intended to alleviate the ban on advertising for lawyers, sworn translators and patent attorneys. The Directive requires, at least in part, the total withdrawal of restrictive prohibitions relating to commercial communications by regulated professions. It is not intended here to be a language concerning the content of commercial communications, but rather a language which generally prohibits the use of one or more forms of commercial communication for a particular profession. For example, it is forbidden to preach in one or more media. According to the explanatory memorandum to the draft law, advertising is in principle permitted, but certain conditions must be respected. Unlike today, a lawyer can use radio, newspapers, and the Internet to promote his activities. According to the new wording, advertising containing an invitation to conclude a contract is completely prohibited. The lawyer must not take advantage of the knowledge that there are persons to whom he can offer legal advice or representation on a case-by-case basis. For example, it is prohibited for a lawyer to use advertising to attract persons who may have claims for damages in certain specific circumstances or to provide them with a contractual representation. So we can no longer ask whether a ban on advertising is good or bad, just regulation needs to be updated. At EU level, this has been resolved by the decision to liberalise the market and authorise advertising. At the same time, the liberalisation of the category often raises a lot of questions. Good coalition ambassadors! On the one hand, you want to liberalise the law by saying eloquently and clearly that it is only because of this that it is possible to offer services and products to a large number of potential customers. On the other hand, however, you claim that imposing restrictions on advertising sales will in no way affect the consumption of services or products. I am thinking here of restrictions on alcohol advertising initiated on the initiative of the Centre Party. I'll have three more minutes, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U391	Three minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U392	Various studies have clearly indicated that the effect of alcohol advertising is reflected in statistics on drinking people, especially young people. As in the case of advertising, it is directly intended to attract the purchase of the product. Estonia's overly free, perhaps even non-existent restrictions on alcohol advertising have obviously had an impact on the consumption patterns of our small people. According to the Institute of Economics, last year the consumption of Estonian people's alcoholic beverages was 18.29 million litres in 100% alcohol, and this averages 10.2 litres of absolute alcohol per capita, from infants to the elderly. If we decide on the basis of the data of their institute of economics, we can claim that the consumption of alcohol in Estonia is self-destructive from the point of view of our people's gene pool. However, I would ask you to give some thought to all these decisions in this Chamber, whether they are statesmanlike or not. The coalition still makes a great deal of harmful decisions. ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U404	President-in-Office of the Council. Ladies and gentlemen, The Prison Act is one of those laws that raise the profile of the population. The main reason for this attention is, of course, the problems of social justice. It is one of the painful issues on the agenda in our country that is intertwined with the law of imprisonment. That's the question: "Show or be"? In other words, Estonia has really started to become a so-called "facade country," where everything is fine on paper, even if this appearance is to be paid for a lot of money. The point is that rich countries can afford the same standard of living, while poor countries can afford a different standard of living. Our government has decided that we are already at the forefront. We're already in the top five, and we're willing to pay it some crazy money sometimes. In the crowd, quietly but sometimes loudly expressed resentment has been caused by the fact that people think that the government loves prisoners more than its poor and old people. And they don't really think that for no reason. Our government strives to treat prisoners as mercifully as we really are among the five richest countries. Such problems have occurred in other countries, but mostly developed European countries have remained dignified. I will give you an example of Norwegian prison practice. For a long time, the Norwegian Parliament did not allow colour televisions to be taken to Norwegian prisons, and black and white televisions were driven through. The reason was simple: prisoners who have been deprived of their liberty for violations of the law deserve no greater privileges than free but poor people. It is true that Norwegian prisons now have colour televisions because they are quite present in the poorest Norwegian families. There are stories in Estonia about how someone asked a police officer, and they do not have undiscovered crimes, some of which could declare themselves to be the perpetrator of it, so that they could move from their unheated and poor habitat to a warm prison for colder months. It's possible there's a humour in this story, or I think it's even irony mixed with reality. There are similar examples of real life. For example, the Estonian media were attracted to the case when the prisoner released from the prison in Tartu refused to leave the prison after the penalty period had passed. Eventually, the man even lay down on the prison entrance and had to call the police to take the former prisoner out of the prison house. The man just didn't have a place where he'd be as safe as living in prison. Full of guts, a roof over your head, what else can you desire? There are many such reproaches. A question that has been through the press, which I find quite interesting and very strange, applies, for example, to Communion wine. Such examples can be found in yes and in more. I'd like three more minutes.

In the debate on Bill 263, do the representatives of the political groups wish to negotiate? Please ask Priit Toobali, representative of the Estonian Central Party Group, to the Riigikogu Chair of Speech!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U426	Dear Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu, Ladies and gentlemen, The draft law amending the Public Information Act aims to stimulate the creation of new products and services in the private sector by making public information re-usable. To this end, the Estonian legal order is specified by the provisions on the re-use of public information. As I said at second reading, the draft was initiated in order to correct the violations referred to by the European Commission in the Estonian legal order. The Commission has indicated that the rights related to the re-use of public information are not guaranteed in Estonia or that the relevant solutions are not sufficiently clear in the current law. It can therefore be reiterated that the wider purpose of the draft is to resolve the problems referred to by the European Commission and to lay down more clearly the rules for the re-use of public information in the Estonian legal order. In fact, Estonia is late in the creation of this legal order or, rather, in the restoration of this legal order, so that the European Commission has repeatedly been asked for an extension period. The European Commission has so far met us, and now it is time to fix this in order to look good in front of the Estonian people and the European Commission. We are very pleased that the Committee on Constitutional Affairs sent its proposals on 263 Estonian Information Technology and Telecommunications Association. Outside the sessions, the Estonian Association of NGOs, the Ministry of Justice and the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications were also met. Four amendments to the draft came together. All these changes are, in my opinion, extremely important. I'll give you some reasons. The first amendment clearly spells out the prohibition of exclusive agreements, unless they are necessary and justified in the public interest. When concluding exclusive agreements, the administrative body must assess each time their necessity and justification. The second amendment was due to negotiations with the Estonian Association for Information Technology and Telecommunications. According to this, the data must be made available in a timely manner. The term 'up-to-date' must be interpreted flexibly. The aim is to keep up as fresh data as possible. This is undoubtedly an important change, because in this way we defend ourselves against the so-called pile-up and upkeep, or forgetting, of garbage. Old data must be clearly distinguishable from up-to-date data. The third amendment was also proposed by the Estonian Information Technology and Telecommunications Association. This deficit could otherwise have been overlooked by the Commission. The possibility of connecting databases is created so that they can be linked to the information gate, to be easier to say, to allow access through a single portal, such as the eesti.ee information gate. By making this amendment, we will avoid the imminent reopening of the law and get a more accurate result. The fourth amendment may be the most important for people, data users. According to the draft law, the law is accompanied by an implementing provision according to which, after the entry into force of § 582 of the Act, everyone must have access free of charge to the data contained in the database to be established after the entry into force of § 582 of the Act. True, this free access must be guaranteed by January 2015, but this too. At the second reading of the draft, I stressed from this panel that all data should be available free of charge and that the cost-based compromise that has been made also raised questions in the Commission. It should be noted that this money is not being asked in other European countries. The draft law amending the Public Information Act, which bears the number 263, is, in my opinion, a good example of how a complex law can be improved by involving different parties, including non-parliamentary parties. Can I have two more minutes?
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U427	It's okay. Two minutes, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U428	In the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, there were very many disputes on this subject, but fortunately our Member of the Commission, a good colleague, Mr Korobeinik, was able to effectively manage the whole process. Various parties were met, as I said: Estonian Information Technology and Telecommunications Union, including the Estonian Internet Community. All meetings were attended by representatives of both the Ministry of Justice and the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications. The corobeian called the various parties several times and was able to transport this rather specific dispute. Why am I talking about this? This shows once again that there are people in coalition parties and even in the Reform Party who want and can reach compromises. Why is this talk of compromise important today? Because restoring Parliament's working peace is also a matter of compromise. It would be extremely easy to restore a cease-fire if coalition parties were more willing to compromise. The proposal of the Centre Party to restore a working peace is a compromise proposal and there is nothing complicated about it. We will lower the electricity excise to ease the rise in electricity prices next year! We have presented our vision, but we are undoubtedly prepared to negotiate all terms with the coalition. We are open and prepared for compromise, just as this draft came about as a result of compromises. However, as far as the Public Information Act is concerned, it should become quite a good law after the draft has entered into force, thanks to numerous discussions. The Centre Party Group intends to support this draft. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U434	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, I hope that the mild cold will not distract me from explaining to you why, in a situation where I would basically like to support the fight against tobacco, I will not do so and will not recommend it to the members of my group. So this is a draft law amending the Law on excise duties on alcohol, tobacco, fuel and electricity, which will increase excise duties on tobacco by 6% next year and the next year. There is such information in the explanatory memorandum that we need to increase the excise duty rate by 10% in order to meet the entry into force of the European Union minimum rate. Firstly, in order to increase 10% in 2014, 6% and 6% will be increased in two years, which is not 10% and not 12% in the end. Why don't I funnyly support any bill coming from the Government of the Republic? Because even if you think everything's okay, sooner or later, it turns out that the skin is being pulled over the ears again. Some time ago, I learned that the European Union has authorised eight countries, including Estonia, to postpone the introduction of this minimum rate until 2018. This means that the increase in the minimum rates of excise duty could be quite different, for example by 1% each year. By 2018, we would achieve these increases by summing up more or less the required result. Instead, quite a lot is being raised in two years, and it is done in such a way that six times more money coming from excise duty is being withdrawn from the wallets of Estonian citizens than could be taken into account by people's interests. It's a story that might sound at the end, and I'll probably repeat that in some way. But before I got this information about 2018, I had a speech prepared that I was about to start. When it comes to the harmful effects of tobacco, the World Health Organization has in some way calculated that in the 20th century, a hundred million people have died in the world because of the effects of tobacco. And the World Health Organization can predict that in the 21st century, 1 billion people will die as a result of the effects of tobacco. However, there are quite interesting examples that tobacco can actually be useful. Thus, some time ago, ornithologists in England discovered that the nests of birds contain an extraordinary number of cigarette butts. That's the thing that got dealt with. I guess everything has to be dealt with, don't you think, just like we do in this hall, so do they. In short, there was also an investigation into the seemingly unnecessary thing to do, why these bones are in the nests. It turned out that there were no arthropods parasites in the nests with smoked bones. Even whole cigarettes were experimented and placed in bird nests, where arthropods did not disappear from the nests. But when there were bones, they disappeared. So every smoker can be sure that at least his lungs don't have ticks. Tobacco has been used by mankind for 4,000 years, and archaeological excavations have proven that North American Indians used tobacco even then. Before the 15th century, it was unknown to no one in Europe, but it began in Columbus. One of his ship's captains, with his telling last name, Pérez, smoked tobacco a few times in America and became a nicotine addict, for which he was sentenced to three years in prison after returning to Spain. Thus, the tradition of combating tobacco is very old, and from this point of view, it can be recognised that the first organisation to fight tobacco was an Inquisition. Sometimes smoking tobacco ended in death, not just because nicotine had a severe effect. Tobacco was also used in politics. At the end of the 16th century, Sir Walter Raleigh lived in England, who worked on tobacco growing in America and brought it to Europe. Greedy Spaniards wanted to take those cargoes away from him, a battle arose. Sir Walter won that little battle somewhere in the Atlantic. But the result: When he got home, he was punished with the death penalty for violating peace. So tobacco has a very different and long history. I'm afraid I haven't gotten to tobacco excise yet. Sometimes there may be an opportunity to talk a little bit more about this subject. But I would like to conclude by saying: we have been deceived by the explanatory memorandum; we have been given false information. I do not support this bill, and it is precisely because it is a crooked piece of explaining that applies to bill 280. Thank you.

Nevertheless, on 7 November, the wish was announced that this draft should be dealt with quickly and that this urgent procedure could be completed by 5 December if the draft was adopted as a law at third reading. This shows that there is no urgent need to redefine plans, that there is no need to accuse the opposition of not being able to adopt one or the other draft. I am now throwing a large stone into the defense minister Urmas Reinsalu's cabbage garden, who has not come to the first reading with drafts concerning foreign missions, which will end this year. If you learn from older colleagues such as Jürgen Ligi, the changes to the 2012 budget will also be dealt with in the Riigikogu at exactly the speed needed. Now, the contents of the bill itself. We all know that the laws amending the State Budget Act generally specify the costs of the current year of ministries. The draft was brought to the Riigikogu with a number of clarifications, after the first reading these clarifications were added and after the second reading also. In the Commission, we also asked that if there is a final vote today, does this mean that there is no need for more ministries to specify their budget expenditure. The answer is no, that is not what it means. The government and ministers will still have the opportunity to make a distinction, and no one will take that opportunity away from them even if there is a final vote today. The budget shall remain the same in case of additional revenue and expenditure. It is true that the last question was answered by Mr Sester, Chairman of the Committee on Monetary Affairs, that this budget is a wonderful budget, because it is a balanced budget. I don't know, maybe it was eloquent, but this budget is not a balanced budget. After changes, it will remain a budget for the budget deficit, as will be the budget for the coming year. We are saying that we cannot lower one tax or another, for example, we will not be able to reduce the electricity excise duty next year and soften the price increases by that, because otherwise our budget would be out of balance, because the budget is already out of balance. But if we come back to the content of the budget, indeed, one of the most important beams here is that Estonia will make a second contribution to the European Stability Mechanism this year. Already on 2 October, the government decided to allocate EUR 29.5 million from the reserve. This EUR 29,5 million will be added to the first instalment and two additional contributions will be made next year. In fact, the Ministry of Finance has planned three contributions. 29.5 million euros is 5.5 million euros more than would be necessary to reduce the excise duty on all Estonian electricity consumers to the minimum allowed in the European Union. The money needed for the ESM was found, I dare say, overnight. If necessary, we will make one deposit, if necessary, we will make two, and if necessary, we will make three contributions next year. The money is not out of Estonia, at least when it needs to be sent to distressed European countries. The ESM contribution has indeed been included in the 2012 State Budget Act Amendment Act. Three more minutes, please!

ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U503	The next item is the third reading of the draft Act 256 amending the Act on the Organisation of Defence Forces and the Act on the Implementation of the Defence Forces Service Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. I'm opening negotiations. Call chair, Aivar Riisalu, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U504	I'll take eight minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U505	Eight minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U506	Good President! Ladies and gentlemen, When I process all draft national defence laws, my heart roars with joy because, at that point, even in the most foggy and harsh winter or autumn darkness, it is as if the sun shines, even though we process these drafts in the deepest hours of the night. National defence is an area for us where an extraordinary but very important cross-party consensus has been achieved in Estonia. Recently with my colleagues in Germany, several gray hairs came to me, because it is sad to see the old European countries, in particular Germany, which has historically been our strategic partner, give in to their national defence today. I was seriously puzzled by the fact that the German state no longer has a headquarters capable, militaryly speaking, of organising German national protection. In addition, they have drawn up considerable armoured capabilities; their national defence system no longer prioritises military service and other such nuances. They contribute very much to missions that must, of course, be recognised in the case of the Germans, as they did not participate in foreign missions for very long decades. I don't understand why they didn't do it because of their deep shame. Now to Estonian affairs. The draft law amending the Law on the Organisation of the Defence Forces and the Act on the Implementation of the Defence Forces Services Act is, by its very nature, a very important draft law. He introduces some factors which once again confirm that national defence is becoming one of the priorities in Estonia, allowing us to make operational changes when national defence needs it. There was, of course, one problem in the procedure of this bill, which might not be so well understood by the MPs in the Riigikogu as well. If the defence forces are to be used to realise police objectives without the right to use force, does this mean that if someone would want to think differently from the popular positions at the moment, the army will be brought upon him until the shovels are launched? No, there's no such threat. But it is clear that in a small country it is reasonable to use the resource if a country needs to do so, especially if this resource is important to us in the sense that we are working on developing it. If the police need help, they can be confident that the defense forces will be able to support him if he fails to perform his duties in some strange way. Secondly, I would like to mention the subject of cyber defence in this bill. This bill subordinates Estonia to the Commander of the Estonian Cyber Defence Forces, making some changes to its position within the structure of the Defence Forces. I think this is a very sensible step, because Estonia has been so forward-looking and innovative in cyber defence in the world, that Estonia currently also has a NATO cyber defence centre. This means that our own activities in this area have been so purposeful and reasonable that NATO has considered it right to trust us and bring its competence centre to us. In particular, I would like to point out that a unique cyber defence alliance exists in Estonia as one of the key structural elements of national defence. This means that in addition to developing national cyber defence, voluntary competences are also involved. I welcome this double, because it is possible to guarantee national security twice effectively with volunteers. Winston Churchill once said that any reservist is a double citizen. It is very sympathetic for me to have this law, but I think that our party's position too is that if we can create some important new competence that gives us the opportunity to be better than before and does not lead to very large-scale resource-intensive investments, this must be done. To confirm this, I dare say that a special operations centre has been set up in Estonia, which is also directly under the command of the Commander of the Defence Forces. It is important for each country to have its own special forces structure. Speaking of a little more than 20 years of the history of the Estonian Defence Forces, we have matured so much today that we have a large number of professionals who would like to go deep in their military careers and service. A special forcesman from all over the world is a truly respected and beloved military man. Special Forces can also be used properly in cases where the world also needs our modest help in solving problems. Our aim does not have to be to participate in military missions, which are literally difficult, with a large human mass. The existence of a special army gives our defence forces the opportunity to develop themselves and to serve a little better within Estonia. It also gives us the opportunity to be dignified as a country when we are required to intervene quickly, operationally. What is still important and sympathetic for special forces is that they are planned for us in this form of so-called development, is that they can now be used at a higher level, i.e. on various training missions, since they are in any case specialists. I dare say that already now our specialists are doing their job here and there in the world. We sincerely welcome the fact that our people can also realize their wisdom and skills in countries and nations that need our help. In any case, I hope that the parties will continue with the same national defence tactics. I thank everyone who will vote positively in the final vote on this draft. Thank you.
