ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U613	Ladies and gentlemen, I follow the proposal of a good colleague, Aadu Black, and his ambition to always see a more general picture behind the details, and to move in the direction of dealing with harmonious legislation. Maybe I need extra minutes, please, eight minutes!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U614	Three more minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U615	First of all, a few words about the situation we have in Estonia today. The land tax is governed by the Land Tax Act adopted in 1993, the most important implementing acts of which are the procedures for implementing the Land Tax Act and the list of data to be provided by local authorities to the Tax and Customs Centre of the Estonian Tax and Customs Board and the procedures for submitting them. According to them, the land tax is collected by the State and fully paid to the local government after the land is located. The amount of land tax payable by the land owner depends on the price of the use of the land, the rate of the land tax imposed by the municipality, the exemptions and advantages provided for by law, and the additional tax incentives imposed by the municipality under the law. In the summer of 2011, we adopted an amendment to the Land Tax Act in the Riigikogu, the purpose of which was to exempt homeowners from land tax. The amendment is based on the Pro Patria and Res Publica Association of the Party and the programme of the Estonian Reform Party's Government Union for the years 2011 to 2015. According to the law, since 2013, the landowner has been exempted from paying the land tax on the property owned by him or on the property of the land in which he or she is established, provided that the building on that land has its residence according to the population register. The scope of the exemption is up to 0,15 hectares in densely populated areas and up to 2 hectares elsewhere. Domestic owners do not have to submit an application for exemption from the land tax; the exemption must be applied automatically, based on data from national databases. Therefore, in order to apply the exemption, before submitting the data to the Tax and Customs Board, the municipality has to compare the land use data with the population register data and, if the landowner is resident in the taxable country, to determine whether a building corresponding to the characteristics of the building is located on that land. If, on the basis of the foregoing, the exemption can be applied, it is necessary to determine whether the rural unit is located in a densely or diffused area and to calculate the tax-free part accordingly. The calculation of the exemption may be made much more difficult for municipalities in a number of specific cases which may exist in the case of land eligible for the exemption. For example, a plot of land has a combined purpose, i.e. not only residential land but also another purpose to which the exemption does not apply, e.g. 10% is commercial land. The part of the parcel of the butter is located in a dense part of the area, but in a distributed area where different levels of relief are to be applied. A part of the parcel may have a protected zone which provides an additional exemption or concession. If the area under home is more than 0,15 hectares in the case of dense settlements or 2 hectares in the case of diffuse settlements, it is necessary to determine to what extent the protected zone is linked to the so-called "domestic" tax-free land. This cannot be done, however, because the borders of the tax-free country of the home country have not been fixed anywhere. Neither the explanatory memorandum nor the additional material of the draft Land Tax Exemption Act of the homeowners indicated which developments are needed for the implementation of the Act and the costs involved in the creation of these developments. It was pointed out that only the tax authorities need to make some IT changes, which in practice meant that the Estonian Tax and Customs Board included a single data field in the form for submitting the information required from local authorities. During the audit of the National Audit Office, the Ministry of Finance acknowledged that no comprehensive forecasts of the costs needed to implement the Act were made prior to and shortly after the adoption of the Act. The draft law on the exemption of homeowners from land tax was initiated by two political groups of the Riigikogu who submitted it to the Riigikogu proceedings on 16 May 2011. On the next day, the Riigikogu Board sent a draft to the Government of the Republic for an opinion, which supported the draft at its meeting on 26 May. Four days after the meeting of the Government of the Republic, a letter from the Minister of Finance arrived at the Finance Committee of the Riigikogu on 30 May. The Minister of Finance had supported the draft at the meeting of the Government of the Republic, but his letter referred to a number of issues that should be resolved in the future procedure. On the next day, however, the Riigikogu decided to finish the first reading of the draft and send it to further readings, the draft of which was somewhat supplemented. The law was passed, as you remember, on June 16, perhaps exactly one month after it was submitted to the proceedings. Since the draft was initiated in the Riigikogu and not in government agencies, it did not pass the normal round of coordination of drafts, during which different ministries and other relevant authorities could have submitted their written opinions on the draft. The Finance Committee of the Riigikogu, which dealt with the bill as a commission, did not ask the Legal and Analytical Department of the Chancellery of the Riigikogu for an assessment of the effects of the legislation, whose competence is to analyse the effects of the draft law. Thus, the Chancellery of the Riigikogu did not analyse the possible impact of this amendment. Today, we process the law amending the Land Tax Act, the Land Tax Act and the Law amending the Income Tax Act 299. The Estonian Centre Party Group has tabled 30 amendments to the draft, of which at present I would like to mention only one and particularly important one. We are still of the opinion that the share of income tax received by local authorities should be restored to the pre-cut level of 11,93%. Only then could we talk about compensating for the land tax exemption. Thus, the share of income tax received by the municipality should increase to at least 12,13%. Only in this way would this bill be realised by compensating for the land tax exemption authorised by the government. In fact, however, this percentage should be higher than this. I will give you an example of the city of Narva-Jõesuu. According to the table sent to the members of the Committee on Monetary Affairs, Narva-Jõesuu loses EUR 2556 despite the compensation provided for in the draft in 2014. In order to fully compensate for the land tax exemption, Narva-Jõesuu City, together with the restoration of the amount of income tax received by the municipality, should increase to 12,16% the rate of income tax received by the municipality referred to in Paragraph 5 (1) of the Income Tax Act. That is why we have tabled 30 amendments, one of which addresses this particular problem. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U618	Please, colleague Mailis Reps! Eight minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U619	Honourable Chairwoman of the Riigikogu! Ladies and gentlemen, Members of the Riigikogu! Since perhaps all members of the Riigikogu who closely monitor us via a computer or television have not yet met the audit of the National Audit Office that has just ended, I am happy to introduce it to you. The audit just concluded by the National Audit Office showed that the landowner cannot be certain that the state will charge him the correct amount of the land tax. The National Audit Office identified calculation errors in all audited municipalities, which required the landowner to pay more or less of the correct amount of the land tax. While the calculation errors or the misinterpretability of the law have not led to significant losses for the State, some taxpayers have benefited from unjustified advantages or had to pay higher land tax than necessary. Under the Land Tax Act and its implementing legislation, the Land Tax and Customs Board calculates the land tax on the basis of data provided by local authorities. The National Audit Office analysed the activities of 13 municipalities when submitting the data necessary for calculating the land tax to the Tax and Customs Board and examined whether the state has organised the land tax system in such a way as to ensure that the land tax is properly accounted for and collected. The National Audit Office found that the Estonian Tax and Customs Board, knowing the Ministry of Finance, does not perform its statutory task of verifying the land tax information provided by the municipalities. The responsibility for the accuracy of the calculations necessary to calculate the land tax has been shifted to the municipalities. Although all income from land tax is transferred to the municipal budget, the land tax is a state tax for which the State is responsible for the collection. The State has not provided uniform databases or applications for calculating land tax for the use of municipalities. The costs which must therefore be borne by the municipalities themselves are not reimbursed by the State. According to the Land Tax Act, some land subject to conservation restrictions must be totally or partially exempted from land tax. Although the Ministry of the Environment has established card applications with its administration, they should help to identify the existence and extent of restrictions, but the data there are not always accurate. Despite the use or non-use of these card applications, most municipalities have made a number of errors in the calculation of exemptions or benefits resulting from nature conservation restrictions. The exemption from the tax on homeland from 2013 will bring much more work for municipalities and cities. The draft law on the exemption of land under homeland was developed in the Riigikogu as a matter of urgency. No analysis was carried out as to whether the law was applicable in its planned form at all and what costs it would entail. On the basis of the national registers, the law cannot be applied in its desired form, as the registers are not combined at such a level that the application or non-application of the land tax exemption for each parcel is automatically reflected in the registers. To date, the Land Administration has been entrusted with the task of creating an online information system that will have to be completed by the end of 2014 and can only be used from 2015. Before that, each municipality must find ways to identify landowners whose place of residence according to the population register is situated on the taxable property. Moreover, it must be determined further whether the building corresponding to the characteristics of the dwelling is located on the property. Only then can the tax exemption be calculated. In many cases, money must also probably be spent on various IT applications, which will become completely useless once the national information system is launched. The tax and customs office's land tax reporting system does not allow the calculation of the amount of land tax for municipalities due to the exemption to be excluded from the calculation on the basis of the available data. Just as no one knows exactly how much money and time it takes for municipalities and cities to calculate the benefits and what administrative cost savings might be if municipalities were to exempt from this complex and often manual comparison of register data. The calculation of each land tax exemption or concession has, according to different parties, made the land tax proportional to one of Estonia's highest costs. According to the National Audit Office, the state must consider simplifying the administration of land tax, restructuring the tax in such a way that the data necessary for calculating land tax can automatically be obtained from the state registers by the Tax and Customs Board. The Tax and Customs Board should only be asked to provide information on their tax rate and local tax incentives. The National Audit Office also dealt with the payment of land tax on land that has no owner or known user. The Ministry of Finance will pay land tax on behalf of the State for such countries. The calculation of the land tax for land without owner or user is not regulated anywhere and therefore the amount of the tax cannot be verified. The Ministry of Finance has never wanted to discuss the accuracy of the calculation of land tax for such countries with the municipalities, but has always accepted the calculations submitted. As a result of the audit, the National Audit Office made recommendations to the Minister of Finance, the Director General of the Tax and Customs Board, the Minister of the Environment and the Estonian Association of Cities and the Estonian Association of Rural Governments. Ministers and the Tax and Customs Board focused their responses on the information system MAKIS, which will be in place from 2015 and will solve some of the problems discussed in the report. At the same time, important activities to calculate land tax until 2015, including the calculation of tax incentives for homeowners from 2013 onwards, will continue to be carried out by municipalities and the necessary tax data for the State will remain incomplete. Land tax is the second largest source of income in rural municipalities and cities after personal income tax. All rural municipalities and cities together earned an average of EUR 46,9 million per year from the land tax in 2007-2011. This accounted for 4% of their total revenue and 7% of their tax revenue. Therefore, the audit of the National Audit Office, as a summary of the activities of the National Audit Office, is also very important for us to follow closely here in the draft law procedure. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U627	Mr President, thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, The draft land tax exemption for the land under home is quite crude and hastyly prepared. As the audit of the National Audit Office reveals, the system for collecting land tax is flawed, and the problems with collecting land tax were most encountered in land-use restrictions. Some taxpayers have, as a result of miscalculation, had to pay a higher land tax than necessary or have received unjustified benefits. The state has left quite a lot of work and responsibility to local authorities, including making calculations. As a result, the wrong calculation is largely dependent on the payer of the land tax itself. However, land tax is an important source of income for local authorities, with EUR 47 million per year in 2007. By exempting land under the land tax, it was not analysed whether the national registers even allowed the law to be implemented in its desired form. In order to apply the exemption, the local authority must compare the land use data with the population register before submitting the data to the Tax and Customs Board. If the landowner's place of residence is in the taxable country, it must be explained from the construction register whether there is a building on that land. If, on the basis of the foregoing, the exemption can be granted, it must be determined whether the rural unit is located in a land of dispersal or dense settlement, and then, accordingly, account must be taken of the tax-free part. Neither the explanatory memorandum nor the annexes to the draft law on the exemption of homeowners from land tax mention what developments are needed for the implementation of the law and how much the costs may be for the creation of these applications. In the course of the audit, the Ministry of Finance has recognised that no comprehensive forecasts of the costs needed to implement the Act were made before or after the adoption of the Act. However, the law was already adopted in June 2011. Since the law was initiated in the Riigikogu, not in the Ministry, the law did not pass through the normal circle of coordination of ministries, during which the parties and ministers could complete the draft with their proposals and opinions. The opinion was also not requested from the Chancellery of the Riigikogu, whose competence is to analyse the effects of draft laws. Based on this explanatory memorandum, the amendment was driven by the heavy tax burden on Tallinn's homeowners and the debate was always focused on Tallinn. At the same time, there are no problems with the implementation of the law in Tallinn and there are no problems in other major cities. Problems arise in smaller municipalities, where there are quite a large number of homeowners, both in densely populated and sparsely populated areas, whose properties are predominantly larger than the scope of the exemption. The Minister of Regional Affairs should have pointed out such shortcomings in the process of dealing with the law and sought solutions to the problems. Following the adoption of the legislative amendment, the Ministry of Finance proposed that it is appropriate for the state to set up a state information system for better administration of land tax. This has only been planned by the end of 2014, which means that this information system will not be available before 2015. For so long, local governments will have to find out their own wisdom about the area of the homeowners as well as the area under their home. I also asked the Commission's rapporteur why it was so late to produce the correct information system. Since the coalition agreement already stated in 2011 that such a tax exemption would be granted for the land under homeland, all the efforts to create an information system could have been implemented in good time. Local authorities are once again entrusted with tasks here, but, as usual, the necessary costs will be borne by the municipalities and no funds will be allocated for these tasks. This, of course, was confirmed by the Commission's rapporteur. One sad consequence is that the tax exemption for the land under home is accompanied by an increase in the price of up to 2.5% per year for land taxation in the municipalities. Unfortunately, the first victim of an increase in land tax is farmers, who are again forced to pay more land tax, because, as a rule, councils have already taken such decisions quickly. This increase in land tax is permitted by § 5 (1) of the Land Tax Act. One of the major shortcomings of this law, which the National Audit Office pointed out, is that the land tax does not have to be paid for the land occupied by the public. If part of the land owned by the landowner is in public use, this part of the land tax must be exempted. However, the law does not specify what to mean by public use. The local authorities also have a legitimate question as to whether the land tax should be exempted from the private land occupied by the public road or from the land under the sandy beach in public use. It has been established that the calculation will cost the owner more than the land tax payable on that land. At the same time, these issues increase the administrative burden on local authorities. Once again, we find that if the municipality does not have this kind of support in the form of an information system, it will take more effort to find out the data itself. So, in fact, this law is well crude and not properly prepared. I'm sure you can't be in favour of this law. Thank you for your attention.

First of all, the share of personal income tax received by the municipality from the second supplementary budget in 2009 was reduced from 11,93% to 11,4%, followed by a tax exemption for domestic land as of 1 January 2013. Now we are seriously talking about compensating for the loss of income from local authorities, so that the share of income tax received by municipalities increases by only 0.17 percentage points, although it was reduced by 0.53 percentage points before. My mathematics says that in order to compensate for cuts made to local governments, the share of income tax received by municipalities in 2013 must be increased by at least 0.17 percentage points plus 0.53 percentage points, or 0.7 percentage points. In this case, however, local authorities will not be compensated for the downfalls of previous years, but the current injustice will end. Directly as a result of the Government's policy, a large part of local governments are currently facing quite serious financial difficulties. Therefore, it is very difficult for both rural municipalities and cities not to say that it is impossible to fulfil all the obligations imposed on them by law. At national level, the problem has still not been either recognised or deliberately ignored. The government coalition is proud of the low tax and debt burden of the Estonian state, but the government departments must report to themselves that this seemingly low debt and tax burden has been achieved largely at the expense of local governments. In a particularly difficult situation, municipalities are below the average. The ranking of the local authorities' capacity index recently published shows that the level and possibilities of Estonian municipalities vary repeatedly, depending on size, location and other criteria. At the top of the ranking, the towns and municipalities of Harjumaa are predominantly enthroned, but the coping and future prospects of small municipalities, for example, in the southeast and eastern part of Estonia, are anything but delightful. Of course, small municipalities, such as the municipality of Põltsamaa or the city of Põlva, are also well-off, but they are more of an exception, thanks to their location, history and also good management, who have been able to find their niche. In general, local authorities in Estonia have reached a critical limit or below the administrative capacity of today, which, in addition to the decisions of the government coalition, has been exacerbated by the decline and concentration of the population in the centres, as well as by the ongoing recession. At times, it is the impression that the aim is to make the financial situation of local authorities worse consciously, so that they are more than willing to accept, as the last straw, the administrative reform proposed by the government coalition, which, at least on paper, allows their financial capacity to be increased. I very much hope that I am wrong here. When I come back to one of the drafts discussed earlier today, I was given the hope of Sven Sester, Chairman of the Committee on Monetary Affairs, who said that the financial autonomy of the municipalities must under no circumstances be reduced in his time. To avoid misunderstanding, I would stress that I am certainly not against administrative reform, but I continue to believe that administrative reform must not only be a forced and mechanical combination of numbers. It is most important to create a real-life self-government system in cooperation with local authorities, based on the principles of the European Charter for Local Government. This means that the administrative organisation of Estonia, the tasks assigned to the local government by law, the budgetary possibilities of cities and rural municipalities and the basis for budget revenue formation must be considered at the same time. All administrative units in the country must be successful and financially insured and independent in their decisions. Just as the state budget must be put together on the basis of long-term objectives and national priorities, the legislative changes and reforms concerning local authorities must be more than one book-based Excel table. Much more important than the figures and technical statistics are the daily livelihood of our people and the prospects for the future. The goal of the government must not be to create weak and easily affected municipalities, but to have a sustainable presence of real-life rural municipalities and cities. The entire Estonian people cannot concentrate only on Harjumaa or, worse, go to Finland at all. Reliable living conditions must be guaranteed throughout Estonia. This means jobs, kindergartens, schools, proper infrastructure, social welfare institutions, cultural institutions, etc. at the same time. Local power is closest to man and therefore all cuts and unexpected changes are most immediately felt there. Any tax policy reforms must aim to improve people's well-being and quality of life. The same principle must also apply to the reform of the Land Tax Act and the Income Tax Act. Unfortunately, I am not sure whether these decisions will result in an increase in the security and security of every person in Estonia and improve the quality of the services provided in cities and municipalities. The state and local authorities must be mutually good partners, not competitors. This means that all laws must be consulted with representatives of local governments and their assessment actually taken into account. It is not appropriate for a democratic state to have a force policy and to be guided by the opinion of its own party leadership alone. I am pleased that at the moment there is also a coalition politician, Valdo Randpere, who, in my opinion, also has a very good local government background, from the City Council of Tallinn. I think he agrees with such a regional, strong local government in Estonia. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U652	Thank you very much. Please, Mr Eldar Efendiev!
ParlaMint-EE_2012-12-05_U653	Madam President! Ladies and gentlemen, We have before us the second reading of bill 299. The previous speakers raised many and thorough arguments, which is why the draft is not good. I don't want to repeat that. But I see it is useful to provide you with information on the situation with local taxes in other European countries. Of course, there are traditions and experiences in different countries, but the Charter of Local Government provides the main directions that will lead to unification in the future. The basic ideology relates to the maximum support for local authorities' ability to provide public services to the local population. But now the examples. Great Britain has strong and long-standing traditions in the activities of local governments and also in cooperation with their central authority. There is a general property tax (NNDR). Local authorities charge real estate tax (UBR), including land tax, which is fully received by the local government. Taxes are quite high and they account for nearly 30% of municipal revenues. France. Since the French Revolution, local land taxes are important in the tax system. The sums received from the land tax are shared between the local and the central authorities and their parts are set by law. The municipality can use registration standards here as well as a double coefficient. It may also grant benefits to persons and to various social groups. It is very interesting that when problems of competence arose between the central authority and the local government, between regions and agencies, they were already solved at national level in 1982 for the benefit of the local government. The competences and benefits were balanced, not in the whole area, but in most of France's territory. Germany. Real estate taxes (Grundsteuer), including land tax, are local taxes. A general federal level of 0.35% has been established, but local authorities can use local coefficients to 280-600%. Holland. Real estate taxes (onroerende zaakbelasting), including land tax, are decided annually by municipalities. The base base shall be provided by the central authority and the local authority may use a coefficient in the calculation, which may vary every year according to the needs of the municipality. Spain. Land taxes, like other local taxes, are annual local taxes. The basis is the prices of the property register confirmed by the central authority, which is reviewed by the central authority every eight years. Switzerland. There are no general federal, central authority-level taxes in this area and all issues are resolved either at cantonal or municipal level. Municipalities have quite a chance to fix taxes. Switzerland is known to be the country with the lowest tax burden compared to other European countries. The European Charter for Local Government highlights four... I'm asking for more time!
